I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m still sort of landing in the same place. I can’t really think of a clear time where I felt sad and there was just… nothing attached to it.

Like, sometimes I’ll think I’m just feeling low, but then if I sit with it for two seconds I realize my mind is on some tiny thing, like whether I forgot to reply to someone or if I sounded weird in a meeting. And then I start thinking about the meeting itself, and who was in it, and whether I should have said something differently, and suddenly it’s not really “nothing” anymore.

So I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean example where I was down without any specific thought or worry tied to it.

I mean, I have bad moments where I feel kind of weighed down, but it usually feels connected to whatever I’m stuck thinking about.\n\nLike, if I’m behind on something or I think I messed up at work, then yeah, I can feel pretty low. But “most of the day, nearly every day”… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. Sometimes I’ll be fine for a bit and then I’ll remember something I was supposed to handle and it’s like my mood just drops. So… I don’t know if that counts as depressed or just me getting caught up in things.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to look forward to, like hobbies, spending time with friends, or activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think I already kind of said this, but it’s hard for me to tell. Like, I still say yes to things sometimes, and I’ll go see friends or watch something, but then my mind is somewhere else and I’m not really… present.\n\nSo I’m not sure if I’ve lost interest, exactly. It’s more like I can’t tell whether I’m enjoying it in the moment because I’m thinking about other stuff. Like even with hobbies, I might start something and then remember a message I didn’t answer, and then the whole thing sort of derails. But I wouldn’t say I don’t care about those things anymore. I just—yeah, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a number of things, like work, school, health, or other everyday matters, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be so concerned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ll start with one thing, like whether I’m behind at work, and then somehow it turns into this whole chain of stuff. Like if I didn’t finish something fast enough, then I’m thinking, okay, what if people think I’m not good at my job, and then I’m thinking about school and whether I’m wasting money trying to do more or be more qualified, and then I’m thinking my family is going to think I can’t handle basic adult things.\n\nAnd it’s not always like there’s some huge problem right in front of me. It can be something small, like an email or a bill or one conversation, but in my head it feels like it could mess up everything, like my whole future somehow. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it doesn’t feel dramatic when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt happy or satisfied, even for a short while, and if so, what caused that feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, maybe there were little moments? Like if something at work gets checked off and I don’t have to keep thinking about it, I feel relieved, but I don’t know if that’s the same as happy or satisfied.\n\nIt’s funny, because people ask that and I start trying to measure it, like, was I actually happy or was I just distracted? And then I end up overthinking the question itself. I did have coffee the other day that was really good, which sounds silly, and for a minute I was like, okay, this is nice. But then I started thinking about whether I was late getting back to something, so it didn’t really last.\n\nSo… I guess yes, short little things, but I can’t think of a big clear example where I felt genuinely happy and just stayed with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… by the middle of the afternoon at the office I feel completely wiped out, even if the day hasn’t been that demanding on paper. Like answering emails starts to feel like this huge task, or even just getting myself to the subway after work feels like too much effort.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I just need the weekend to reset, but it doesn’t really work that way. I can rest, not do much, and I still don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because it makes me feel like I’m being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like a normal tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you're not good enough, even when things haven't necessarily gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly. Like, if something actually goes wrong, then sure, I’ll feel bad about it, but I can’t think of a clear time where I just felt guilty out of nowhere.\n\n“Not good enough” is harder because I don’t always know what counts as that versus just… being hard on myself, I guess. Like I’ll look back at something I said in a meeting and wonder if it came across weird, and then I’m thinking about how other people seem to just talk normally without replaying every sentence—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I notice second-guessing more than guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety, even when you’re not intentionally trying to speed up or slow down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess? I haven’t noticed myself moving slower or anything like that. Speaking slower, maybe if I’m tired, but I don’t think other people have said anything.\n\nRestless is harder because I do… I mean, I’ll adjust in my chair or pick at the edge of a paper sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s just normal office stuff. There’s this one conference room chair that squeaks every time you move, so then I become really aware of every tiny movement, which probably makes me move more, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s been some obvious change where I’m really slowed down or noticeably fidgety. I’m not sure I’d be the best judge, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been times when you didn’t feel like doing anything at all, not because you were distracted or busy thinking, but because nothing really seemed worth the effort or felt enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, there are definitely times I don’t feel like doing things, but I don’t know if it’s because nothing feels worth it, exactly, or if I’m just tired or overthinking how much effort it’ll take.\n\nLike even deciding what to make for dinner can turn into this whole thing, because then I’m thinking about groceries and dishes and whether I bought the spinach that always goes bad in the back of the fridge—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed a clear “nothing is enjoyable” feeling. It’s more that some things feel like a lot to start, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you used to really enjoy, like a hobby, a TV show, or spending time with someone, where now you don’t get the same feeling from it, even when your mind isn’t racing or you’re not distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of one. Like, I still watch shows I like, and if I’m with someone I like being around, I don’t think it feels empty or anything. Sometimes I don’t get into it right away, but then I’m also checking the time or thinking about whether I forgot to send an email, so that probably doesn’t count because you said when my mind isn’t racing.\n\nI used to do this thing where I’d rewatch the same cooking videos, not even to cook, just because the chopping sounds were kind of calming, which sounds weird now that I’m saying it. But I don’t know that I stopped enjoying them exactly. I just forget, or I decide I should be doing something more useful, and then suddenly I’ve reorganized one drawer and made the kitchen look worse.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a clear hobby or person where I’m like, “this doesn’t feel good anymore.” I haven’t noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you didn’t feel any joy or pleasure from something you used to genuinely enjoy, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way. Like, if I watch something I usually like, I might take a while to settle into it, but I wouldn’t say I feel nothing from it.\n\nThe closest thing is maybe I’ll put on a show and then spend half the episode looking up whether the actor was in something else, and then somehow I’m reading about where a movie was filmed in 2008, which is not the same as enjoying the show, obviously. But that’s more me getting pulled away, not that it doesn’t feel good anymore.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really think of a recent time where something I genuinely enjoyed just gave me no pleasure at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time in the past couple of weeks when you didn’t feel any sense of pleasure or joy, even from something you used to enjoy, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but I don’t really have one that fits exactly. Like, in the past couple weeks I’ve definitely had moments where I started something I usually like and didn’t immediately feel into it, but then I can’t tell if that’s because I wasn’t enjoying it or because I was half-thinking about ten other little things.\n\nLike I put on a show the other night and paused it three times because I remembered laundry, and then I noticed the plant by the window looked sad, and then I was Googling whether you’re supposed to mist those plants or if that’s actually bad for them—which, apparently, people have very strong opinions about. But when I actually sat back down, I did enjoy parts of the show.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I can point to a specific time where I felt no pleasure at all from something I usually enjoy. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time recently when you were fully focused on something you used to enjoy, without distractions or overthinking, and still didn't feel any pleasure or joy from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can, no. I’m trying to think of something where I was really fully focused, because that’s the tricky part. Usually if I’m doing something I like, I’m also sort of… checking my phone, or remembering I need to order paper towels, or noticing that the baseboards are dusty even though I never notice baseboards any other time.\n\nLike, I did sit with a book for a bit recently, and I thought, “Okay, I’m going to actually read,” but then I got caught up wondering if I’d read another book by the same author, and then I was looking at reviews, which is a whole rabbit hole because people get strangely intense about Goodreads ratings.\n\nBut if you mean I was calmly, fully focused on something I used to enjoy and still felt nothing from it… I don’t think I’ve had that. I haven’t noticed a moment like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad even when there wasn’t anything specific you were worrying about or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve felt kind of low or off before, but usually when I look at it there’s some little thread attached to it, like I was thinking about something I needed to do or replaying some conversation, even if it wasn’t a big obvious thing.\n\nIt’s hard because sometimes I’ll be sitting there and think, “Why do I feel weird?” and then I realize I’ve been mentally organizing my whole week or worrying about whether I answered an email too bluntly. And then I’ll get distracted by, like, the exact wording of the email, which is ridiculous because nobody is probably analyzing it that much.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve had a time where I was just sad with nothing specific in my mind. I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time in the past two weeks when you were fully engaged in something you used to really enjoy and found it completely unpleasurable, even for a moment, not just difficult to start or easy to get distracted from?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to separate “completely unpleasurable” from “I couldn’t settle into it,” because those feel different to me.\n\nLike, I did try to do a couple things I normally like, but then I’m also thinking, did I actually give it a fair chance? I’ll be watching something and then suddenly I’m checking whether I paid a bill, or I’ll start a podcast and then realize I’ve rewound the same part three times because I was thinking about something else. And then I get annoyed because now I don’t know if the podcast was boring or if my brain just wandered off.\n\nBut a time where I was really fully engaged, like actually absorbed, and it felt completely empty or not pleasurable at all… no, I can’t think of a specific moment like that in the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you weren’t distracted or caught up in thinking about other things, but still didn’t feel any pleasure or joy from something you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think I can come up with a clean example of that. It’s like… if I’m doing something I used to enjoy, I’m almost always also half-thinking about something else, or I interrupt myself.\n\nLike even if I sit down to watch a show, I’ll suddenly wonder if I responded to someone, and then I’ll check, and then I’ll notice some other thing on my phone, and then somehow I’m looking at storage baskets online, which is not even what I meant to do. I did that recently and then got annoyed because I couldn’t tell if I wasn’t enjoying the show or if I’d just ruined it by multitasking.\n\nSo if you mean no distractions, no overthinking, and still no pleasure… I haven’t really noticed a moment like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad even when there wasn’t anything specific you were worrying about or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m still sort of landing in the same place. I can’t really think of a clear time where I felt sad and there was just… nothing attached to it.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll think I’m just feeling low, but then if I sit with it for two seconds I realize my mind is on some tiny thing, like whether I forgot to reply to someone or if I sounded weird in a meeting. And then I start thinking about the meeting itself, and who was in it, and whether I should have said something differently, and suddenly it’s not really “nothing” anymore.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean example where I was down without any specific thought or worry tied to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s like… if I’m doing something I used to enjoy, I’m almost always also half-thinking about something else, or I interrupt myself.\n\nLike even if I sit down to watch a show, I’ll suddenly wonder if I responded to someone, and then I’ll check, and then I’ll notice some other thing on my phone, and then somehow I’m looking at storage baskets online, which is not even what I meant to do. I did that recently and then got annoyed because I couldn’t tell if I wasn’t enjoying the show or if I’d just ruined it by multitasking.\n\nSo if you mean no distractions, no overthinking, and still no pleasure… I haven’t really noticed a moment like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad even when there wasn’t anything specific you were worrying about or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m still sort of landing in the same place. I can’t really think of a clear time where I felt sad and there was just… nothing attached to it.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll think I’m just feeling low, but then if I sit with it for two seconds I realize my mind is on some tiny thing, like whether I forgot to reply to someone or if I sounded weird in a meeting. And then I start thinking about the meeting itself, and who was in it, and whether I should have said something differently, and suddenly it’s not really “nothing” anymore.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean example where I was down without any specific thought or worry tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad, not because of something specific you were thinking about, but just because that’s how you felt, even when your mind was quiet and there wasn’t anything particular on your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to find a moment like that, where my mind was actually quiet, and I don’t really know that I’ve had one.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and think, “Why do I feel so off?” but then immediately I’m like, oh, I’m thinking about that email, or what I said earlier, or whether I forgot some little thing. And then I start tracing it back, which probably doesn’t help because then I’m basically making a whole project out of figuring out why I feel bad.\n\nSo… if you mean just sad out of nowhere, with nothing on my mind, I haven’t really noticed that. I can’t give you a clear example.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I did that recently and then got annoyed because I couldn’t tell if I wasn’t enjoying the show or if I’d just ruined it by multitasking.\n\nSo if you mean no distractions, no overthinking, and still no pleasure… I haven’t really noticed a moment like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad even when there wasn’t anything specific you were worrying about or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m still sort of landing in the same place. I can’t really think of a clear time where I felt sad and there was just… nothing attached to it.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll think I’m just feeling low, but then if I sit with it for two seconds I realize my mind is on some tiny thing, like whether I forgot to reply to someone or if I sounded weird in a meeting. I haven’t noticed a clean example where I was down without any specific thought or worry tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad, not because of something specific you were thinking about, but just because that’s how you felt, even when your mind was quiet and there wasn’t anything particular on your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to find a moment like that, where my mind was actually quiet, and I don’t really know that I’ve had one.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and think, “Why do I feel so off?” but then immediately I’m like, oh, I’m thinking about that email, or what I said earlier, or whether I forgot some little thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not really coming up with one, no. I keep getting stuck on the “mind was quiet” part, because I don’t know that I notice that very often.

Like, I might feel kind of heavy or blah, but then I’ll realize I’m thinking about something stupidly specific, like whether I came across wrong in a message, or if I forgot to do some errand. And then my brain goes off on that whole chain — like I’ll remember I need laundry detergent, and then I’m thinking about the closet being a mess, and then I’m annoyed at myself for not being more organized — and by then it’s hard to say what the original feeling even was.

So I guess… no, I can’t really point to a time where I was just sad with nothing particular on my mind.

Like if I didn’t finish something fast enough, then I’m thinking, okay, what if people think I’m not good at my job, and then I’m thinking about school and whether I’m wasting money trying to do more or be more qualified, and then I’m thinking my family is going to think I can’t handle basic adult things.\n\nAnd it’s not always like there’s some huge problem right in front of me. It can be something small, like an email or a bill or one conversation, but in my head it feels like it could mess up everything, like my whole future somehow. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it doesn’t feel dramatic when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt happy or satisfied, even for a short while, and if so, what caused that feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, maybe there were little moments? Like if something at work gets checked off and I don’t have to keep thinking about it, I feel relieved, but I don’t know if that’s the same as happy or satisfied.\n\nIt’s funny, because people ask that and I start trying to measure it, like, was I actually happy or was I just distracted? And then I end up overthinking the question itself. I did have coffee the other day that was really good, which sounds silly, and for a minute I was like, okay, this is nice. But then I started thinking about whether I was late getting back to something, so it didn’t really last.\n\nSo… I guess yes, short little things, but I can’t think of a big clear example where I felt genuinely happy and just stayed with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… by the middle of the afternoon at the office I feel completely wiped out, even if the day hasn’t been that demanding on paper. Like answering emails starts to feel like this huge task, or even just getting myself to the subway after work feels like too much effort.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I just need the weekend to reset, but it doesn’t really work that way. I can rest, not do much, and I still don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because it makes me feel like I’m being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like a normal tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you're not good enough, even when things haven't necessarily gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly. Like, if something actually goes wrong, then sure, I’ll feel bad about it, but I can’t think of a clear time where I just felt guilty out of nowhere.\n\n“Not good enough” is harder because I don’t always know what counts as that versus just… being hard on myself, I guess. Like I’ll look back at something I said in a meeting and wonder if it came across weird, and then I’m thinking about how other people seem to just talk normally without replaying every sentence—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I notice second-guessing more than guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety, even when you’re not intentionally trying to speed up or slow down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess? I haven’t noticed myself moving slower or anything like that. Speaking slower, maybe if I’m tired, but I don’t think other people have said anything.\n\nRestless is harder because I do… I mean, I’ll adjust in my chair or pick at the edge of a paper sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s just normal office stuff. There’s this one conference room chair that squeaks every time you move, so then I become really aware of every tiny movement, which probably makes me move more, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s been some obvious change where I’m really slowed down or noticeably fidgety. I’m not sure I’d be the best judge, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been times when you didn’t feel like doing anything at all, not because you were distracted or busy thinking, but because nothing really seemed worth the effort or felt enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, there are definitely times I don’t feel like doing things, but I don’t know if it’s because nothing feels worth it, exactly, or if I’m just tired or overthinking how much effort it’ll take.\n\nLike even deciding what to make for dinner can turn into this whole thing, because then I’m thinking about groceries and dishes and whether I bought the spinach that always goes bad in the back of the fridge—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed a clear “nothing is enjoyable” feeling. It’s more that some things feel like a lot to start, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you used to really enjoy, like a hobby, a TV show, or spending time with someone, where now you don’t get the same feeling from it, even when your mind isn’t racing or you’re not distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of one. Like, I still watch shows I like, and if I’m with someone I like being around, I don’t think it feels empty or anything. Sometimes I don’t get into it right away, but then I’m also checking the time or thinking about whether I forgot to send an email, so that probably doesn’t count because you said when my mind isn’t racing.\n\nI used to do this thing where I’d rewatch the same cooking videos, not even to cook, just because the chopping sounds were kind of calming, which sounds weird now that I’m saying it. But I don’t know that I stopped enjoying them exactly. I just forget, or I decide I should be doing something more useful, and then suddenly I’ve reorganized one drawer and made the kitchen look worse.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a clear hobby or person where I’m like, “this doesn’t feel good anymore.” I haven’t noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you didn’t feel any joy or pleasure from something you used to genuinely enjoy, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way. Like, if I watch something I usually like, I might take a while to settle into it, but I wouldn’t say I feel nothing from it.\n\nThe closest thing is maybe I’ll put on a show and then spend half the episode looking up whether the actor was in something else, and then somehow I’m reading about where a movie was filmed in 2008, which is not the same as enjoying the show, obviously. But that’s more me getting pulled away, not that it doesn’t feel good anymore.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really think of a recent time where something I genuinely enjoyed just gave me no pleasure at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time in the past couple of weeks when you didn’t feel any sense of pleasure or joy, even from something you used to enjoy, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but I don’t really have one that fits exactly. Like, in the past couple weeks I’ve definitely had moments where I started something I usually like and didn’t immediately feel into it, but then I can’t tell if that’s because I wasn’t enjoying it or because I was half-thinking about ten other little things.\n\nLike I put on a show the other night and paused it three times because I remembered laundry, and then I noticed the plant by the window looked sad, and then I was Googling whether you’re supposed to mist those plants or if that’s actually bad for them—which, apparently, people have very strong opinions about. But when I actually sat back down, I did enjoy parts of the show.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I can point to a specific time where I felt no pleasure at all from something I usually enjoy. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time recently when you were fully focused on something you used to enjoy, without distractions or overthinking, and still didn't feel any pleasure or joy from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can, no. I’m trying to think of something where I was really fully focused, because that’s the tricky part. Usually if I’m doing something I like, I’m also sort of… checking my phone, or remembering I need to order paper towels, or noticing that the baseboards are dusty even though I never notice baseboards any other time.\n\nLike, I did sit with a book for a bit recently, and I thought, “Okay, I’m going to actually read,” but then I got caught up wondering if I’d read another book by the same author, and then I was looking at reviews, which is a whole rabbit hole because people get strangely intense about Goodreads ratings.\n\nBut if you mean I was calmly, fully focused on something I used to enjoy and still felt nothing from it… I don’t think I’ve had that. I haven’t noticed a moment like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad even when there wasn’t anything specific you were worrying about or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve felt kind of low or off before, but usually when I look at it there’s some little thread attached to it, like I was thinking about something I needed to do or replaying some conversation, even if it wasn’t a big obvious thing.\n\nIt’s hard because sometimes I’ll be sitting there and think, “Why do I feel weird?” and then I realize I’ve been mentally organizing my whole week or worrying about whether I answered an email too bluntly. And then I’ll get distracted by, like, the exact wording of the email, which is ridiculous because nobody is probably analyzing it that much.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve had a time where I was just sad with nothing specific in my mind. I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time in the past two weeks when you were fully engaged in something you used to really enjoy and found it completely unpleasurable, even for a moment, not just difficult to start or easy to get distracted from?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to separate “completely unpleasurable” from “I couldn’t settle into it,” because those feel different to me.\n\nLike, I did try to do a couple things I normally like, but then I’m also thinking, did I actually give it a fair chance? I’ll be watching something and then suddenly I’m checking whether I paid a bill, or I’ll start a podcast and then realize I’ve rewound the same part three times because I was thinking about something else. And then I get annoyed because now I don’t know if the podcast was boring or if my brain just wandered off.\n\nBut a time where I was really fully engaged, like actually absorbed, and it felt completely empty or not pleasurable at all… no, I can’t think of a specific moment like that in the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you weren’t distracted or caught up in thinking about other things, but still didn’t feel any pleasure or joy from something you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think I can come up with a clean example of that. It’s like… if I’m doing something I used to enjoy, I’m almost always also half-thinking about something else, or I interrupt myself.\n\nLike even if I sit down to watch a show, I’ll suddenly wonder if I responded to someone, and then I’ll check, and then I’ll notice some other thing on my phone, and then somehow I’m looking at storage baskets online, which is not even what I meant to do. I did that recently and then got annoyed because I couldn’t tell if I wasn’t enjoying the show or if I’d just ruined it by multitasking.\n\nSo if you mean no distractions, no overthinking, and still no pleasure… I haven’t really noticed a moment like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad even when there wasn’t anything specific you were worrying about or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m still sort of landing in the same place. I can’t really think of a clear time where I felt sad and there was just… nothing attached to it.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll think I’m just feeling low, but then if I sit with it for two seconds I realize my mind is on some tiny thing, like whether I forgot to reply to someone or if I sounded weird in a meeting. And then I start thinking about the meeting itself, and who was in it, and whether I should have said something differently, and suddenly it’s not really “nothing” anymore.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean example where I was down without any specific thought or worry tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad, not because of something specific you were thinking about, but just because that’s how you felt, even when your mind was quiet and there wasn’t anything particular on your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to find a moment like that, where my mind was actually quiet, and I don’t really know that I’ve had one.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and think, “Why do I feel so off?” but then immediately I’m like, oh, I’m thinking about that email, or what I said earlier, or whether I forgot some little thing. And then I start tracing it back, which probably doesn’t help because then I’m basically making a whole project out of figuring out why I feel bad.\n\nSo… if you mean just sad out of nowhere, with nothing on my mind, I haven’t really noticed that. I can’t give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt down or sad, not because of something specific you were thinking about, but just because that’s how you felt, even when your mind was quiet and there wasn’t anything particular on your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really coming up with one, no. I keep getting stuck on the “mind was quiet” part, because I don’t know that I notice that very often.\n\nLike, I might feel kind of heavy or blah, but then I’ll realize I’m thinking about something stupidly specific, like whether I came across wrong in a message, or if I forgot to do some errand. And then my brain goes off on that whole chain — like I’ll remember I need laundry detergent, and then I’m thinking about the closet being a mess, and then I’m annoyed at myself for not being more organized — and by then it’s hard to say what the original feeling even was.\n\nSo I guess… no, I can’t really point to a time where I was just sad with nothing particular on my mind."
